[Narrator] I She stood on the platform watching the receding train. [/]
[Narrator] A few bushes hid the curve of the line; the white vapour rose above them, evaporating in the pale evening. [/]
[Narrator] A moment more and the last carriage would pass out of sight. [/]
[Narrator] The white gates swung forward slowly and closed over the line. [/]
[Narrator] An oblong box painted reddish brown and tied with a rough rope lay on the seat beside her. [/]
[Narrator] The movement of her back and shoulders showed that the bundle she carried was a heavy one, the sharp bulging of the grey linen cloth that the weight was dead. [/]
[Narrator] She wore a faded yellow dress and a black jacket too warm for the day. [/]
[Narrator] A girl of twenty, short, strongly built, with short, strong arms. [/]
[Narrator] Her neck was plump, and her hair of so ordinary a brown that it passed unnoticed. [/]
[Narrator] The nose was too thick, but the nostrils were well formed. [/]
[Narrator] The eyes were grey, luminous, and veiled with dark lashes. [/]
[Narrator] But it was only when she laughed that her face lost its habitual expression, which was somewhat sullen; then it flowed with bright humour. [/]
[Narrator] She laughed now, showing a white line of almond - shaped teeth. [/]
[Narrator] The porter had asked her if she were afraid to leave her bundle with her box. [/]
[Narrator] Both, he said, would go up together in the donkey - cart. [/]
[Narrator] The donkey - cart came down every evening to fetch parcels. .. . [/]
[Narrator] That was the way to Woodview, right up the lane. [/]
[Narrator] She could not miss it. [/]
[Narrator] She would find the lodge gate in that clump of trees. [/]
[Narrator] The man lingered, for she was an attractive girl, but the station - master called him away to remove some luggage. [/]
[Narrator] It was a barren country. [/]
[Narrator] Once the sea had crawled at high tide half - way up the sloping sides of those downs. [/]
[Narrator] It would do so now were it not for the shingle bank which its surging had thrown up along the coast. [/]
[Narrator] Between the shingle bank and the shore a weedy river flowed and the little town stood clamped together, its feet in the water's edge. [/]
[Narrator] There were decaying shipyards about the harbour, and wooden breakwaters stretched long, thin arms seawards for ships that did not come. [/]
[Narrator] On the other side of the railway apple blossoms showed above a white - washed wall; some market gardening was done in the low - lying fields, whence the downs rose in gradual ascents. [/]
[Narrator] On the first slope there was a fringe of trees. [/]
[Narrator] That was Woodview. [/]
[Narrator] The girl gazed on this bleak country like one who saw it for the first time. [/]
[Narrator] She saw without perceiving, for her mind was occupied with personal consideration. [/]
[Narrator] She found it difficult to decide whether she should leave her bundle with her box. [/]
[Narrator] It hung heavy in her hand, and she did not know how far Woodview was from the station. [/]
[Narrator] At the end of the platform the station - master took her ticket, and she passed over the level - crossing still undecided. [/]
[Narrator] The lane began with iron railings, laurels, and French windows. [/]
[Narrator] She had been in service in such houses, and knew if she were engaged in any of them what her duties would be. [/]
[Narrator] But the life in Woodview was a great dream, and she could not imagine herself accomplishing all that would be required of her. [/]
[Narrator] There would be a butler, a footman, and a page; she would not mind the page -- but the butler and footman, what would they think? [/]
[Narrator] There would be an upper - housemaid and an under - housemaid, and perhaps a lady's - maid, and maybe that these ladies had been abroad with the family. [/]
[Narrator] She had heard of France and Germany. [/]
[Narrator] Their conversation would, no doubt, turn on such subjects. [/]
[Narrator] Her silence would betray her. [/]
[Narrator] They would ask her what situations she had been in, and when they learned the truth she would have to leave disgraced. [/]
[Narrator] She had not sufficient money to pay for a ticket to London. [/]
[Narrator] But what excuse could she give to Lady Elwin, who had rescued her from Mrs. Dunbar and got her the place of kitchen - maid at Woodview? [/]
[Narrator] She must not go back. [/]
[Narrator] Her father would curse her, and perhaps beat her mother and her too. [/]
[Narrator] Ah! [/]
[Narrator] he would not dare to strike her again, and the girl's face flushed with shameful remembrance. [/]
[Narrator] And her little brothers and sisters would cry if she came back. [/]
[Narrator] They had little enough to eat as it was. [/]
[Narrator] Of course she must not go back. [/]
[Narrator] How silly of her to think of such a thing! [/]
[Narrator] She smiled, and her face became as bright as the month: it was the first day of June. [/]
[Narrator] Still she would be glad when the first week was over. [/]
[Narrator] If she had only a dress to wear in the afternoons! [/]
[Narrator] The old yellow thing on her back would never do. [/]
[Narrator] But one of her cotton prints was pretty fresh; she must get a bit of red ribbon -- that would make a difference. [/]
[Narrator] She had heard that the housemaids in places like Woodview always changed their dresses twice a day, and on Sundays went out in silk mantles and hats in the newest fashion. [/]
[Narrator] As for the lady's - maid, she of course had all her mistress's clothes, and walked with the butler. [/]
[Narrator] What would such people think of a little girl like her! [/]
[Narrator] Her heart sank at the thought, and she sighed, anticipating much bitterness and disappointment. [/]
[Narrator] Even when her first quarter's wages came due she would hardly be able to buy herself a dress: they would want the money at home. [/]
[Narrator] Her quarter's wages! [/]
[Narrator] A month's wages most like, for she'd never be able to keep the place. [/]
[Narrator] No doubt all those fields belonged to the Squire, and those great trees too; they must be fine folk, quite as fine as Lady Elwin -- finer, for she lived in a house like those near the station. [/]
[Narrator] On both sides of the straight road there were tall hedges, and the nursemaids lay in the wide shadows on the rich summer grass, their perambulators at a little distance. [/]
[Narrator] The hum of the town died out of the ear, and the girl continued to imagine the future she was about to enter on with increasing distinctness. [/]
[Narrator] Looking across the fields she could see two houses, one in grey stone, the other in red brick with a gable covered with ivy; and between them, lost in the north, the spire of a church. [/]
[Narrator] On questioning a passer - by she learnt that the first house was the Rectory, the second was Woodview Lodge. [/]
[Narrator] If that was the lodge, what must the house be? [/]
[Narrator] Two hundred yards further on the road branched, passing on either side of a triangular clump of trees, entering the sea road; and under the leaves the air was green and pleasant, and the lungs of the jaded town girl drew in a deep breath of health. [/]
[Narrator] Behind the plantation she found a large white - painted wooden gate. [/]
[Narrator] It opened into a handsome avenue, and the gatekeeper told her to keep straight on, and to turn to the left when she got to the top. [/]
[Narrator] She had never seen anything like it before, and stopped to admire the uncouth arms of elms, like rafters above the roadway; pink clouds showed through, and the monotonous dove seemed the very heart of the silence. [/]
[Narrator] Her doubts returned; she never would be able to keep the place. [/]
[Narrator] The avenue turned a little, and she came suddenly upon a young man leaning over the paling, smoking his pipe. [/]
[AYoungManLeaningOverThePaling] "Please sir, is this the way to Woodview?""Yes, right up through the stables, round to the left." [/]
[AYoungManLeaningOverThePaling] Then, noticing the sturdily - built figure, yet graceful in its sturdiness, and the bright cheeks, he said,"You look pretty well done; that bundle is a heavy one, let me hold it for you. [/]
[Narrator] ""I am a bit tired,"she said, leaning the bundle on the paling. [/]
[Narrator] "They told me at the station that the donkey - cart would bring up my box later on.""Ah, then you are the new kitchen - maid? [/]
[Narrator] What's your name?""Esther Waters.""My mother's the cook here; you'll have to mind your p's and q's or else you'll be dropped on. [/]
[Narrator] The devil of a temper while it lasts, but not a bad sort if you do n't put her out.""Are you in service here?""No, but I hope to be afore long. [/]
[Narrator] I could have been two years ago, but mother did not like me to put on livery, and I do n't know how I'll face her when I come running down to go out with the carriage.""Is the place vacant?" [/]
[Narrator] Esther asked, raising her eyes timidly, looking at him sideways. [/]
[AYoungManLeaningOverThePaling] "Yes, Jim Story got the sack about a week ago. [/]
[AYoungManLeaningOverThePaling] When he had taken a drop he'd tell every blessed thing that was done in the stables. [/]
[AYoungManLeaningOverThePaling] They'd get him down to the'Red Lion'for the purpose; of course the squire could n't stand that. [/]
[AYoungManLeaningOverThePaling] ""And shall you take the place?""Yes. [/]
[AYoungManLeaningOverThePaling] I'm not going to spend my life carrying parcels up and down the King's Road, Brighton, if I can squeeze in here. [/]
[AYoungManLeaningOverThePaling] It is n't so much the berth that I care about, but the advantages, information fresh from the fountain - head. [/]
[AYoungManLeaningOverThePaling] You wo n't catch me chattering over the bar at the'Red Lion'and having every blessed word I say wired up to London and printed next morning in all the papers. [/]
[AYoungManLeaningOverThePaling] "Esther wondered what he was talking about, and, looking at him, she saw a low, narrow forehead, a small, round head, a long nose, a pointed chin, and rather hollow, bloodless cheeks. [/]
[Narrator] Notwithstanding the shallow chest, he was powerfully built, the long arms could deal a swinging blow. [/]
[Narrator] The low forehead and the lustreless eyes told of a slight, unimaginative brain, but regular features and a look of natural honesty made William Latch a man that ten men and eighteen women out of twenty would like. [/]
[He] "I see you have got books in that bundle,"he said at the end of a long silence. [/]
[Narrator] "Fond of readin'?""They are mother's books,"she replied, hastily. [/]
[Narrator] "I was afraid to leave them at the station, for it would be easy for anyone to take one out, and I should not miss it until I undid the bundle.""Sarah Tucker -- that's the upper - housemaid -- will be after you to lend them to her. [/]
[He] She is a wonderful reader. [/]
[He] She has read every story that has come out in _ Bow Bells _ for the last three years, and you ca n't puzzle her, try as you will. [/]
[He] She knows all the names, can tell you which lord it was that saved the girl from the carriage when the'osses were tearing like mad towards a precipice a'undred feet deep, and all about the baronet for whose sake the girl went out to drown herself in the moonlight, I'aven't read the books mesel', but Sarah and me are great pals,"Esther trembled lest he might ask her again if she were fond of reading; she could not read. [/]
[Narrator] Noticing a change in the expression of her face, he concluded that she was disappointed to hear that he liked Sarah and regretted his indiscretion. [/]
[Esther] "Good friends, you know -- no more. [/]
[Esther] Sarah and me never hit it off; she will worry me with the stories she reads. [/]
[Esther] I do n't know what is your taste, but I likes something more practical; the little'oss in there, he is more to my taste." [/]
[Esther] Fearing he might speak again of her books, she mustered up courage and said--"They told me at the station that the donkey - cart would bring up my box." [/]
[Esther] "The donkey - cart is n't going to the station to - night -- you'll want your things, to be sure. [/]
[Esther] I'll see the coachman; perhaps he is going down with the trap. [/]
[Esther] But, golly! [/]
[Esther] it has gone the half - hour. [/]
[Esther] I shall catch it for keeping you talking, and my mother has been expecting you for the last hour. [/]
[Esther] She has n't a soul to help her, and six people coming to dinner. [/]
[Esther] You must say the train was late.""Let us go, then,"cried Esther. [/]
[Esther] "Will you show me the way?"Over the iron gate which opened into the pleasure - ground, thick branches of evergreen oaks made an arch of foliage, and between the trees a glimpse was caught of the angles and urns of an Italian house -- distant about a hundred yards. [/]
[Narrator] A high brick wall separated the pleasure - ground from the stables, and as William and Esther turned to the left and walked up the roadway he explained that the numerous buildings were stables. [/]
[Narrator] They passed by many doors, hearing the trampling of horses and the rattling of chains. [/]
[Narrator] Then the roadway opened into a handsome yard overlooked by the house, the back premises of which had been lately rebuilt in red brick. [/]
[Narrator] There were gables and ornamental porches, and through the large kitchen windows the servants were seen passing to and fro. [/]
[Narrator] At the top of this yard was a gate. [/]
[Narrator] It led into the park, and, like the other gate, was overhung by bunched evergreens. [/]
[Narrator] A string of horses came towards this gate, and William ran to open it. [/]
[Narrator] The horses were clothed in grey cloth. [/]
[Narrator] They wore hoods, and Esther noticed the black round eyes looking through the eyelet holes. [/]
[Narrator] They were ridden by small, ugly boys, who swung their little legs, and struck them with ash plants when they reached their heads forward chawing at the bits. [/]
[William] When William returned he said,"Look there, the third one; that's he -- that's Silver Braid. [/]
[William] "An impatient knocking at the kitchen window interrupted his admiration, and William, turning quickly, said,"Mind you say the train was late; do n't say I kept you, or you'll get me into the devil of a pickle. [/]
[William] This way." [/]
[Narrator] The door let into a wide passage covered with coconut matting. [/]
[Narrator] They walked a few yards; the kitchen was the first door, and the handsome room she found herself in did not conform to anything that Esther had seen or heard of kitchens. [/]
[Narrator] The range almost filled one end of the room, and on it a dozen saucepans were simmering; the dresser reached to the ceiling, and was covered with a multitude of plates and dishes. [/]
[Narrator] Esther thought how she must strive to keep it in its present beautiful condition, and the elegant white - capped servants passing round the white table made her feel her own insignificance. [/]
[TheElegantWhiteCappedServantsPassingRoundTheWhiteTable] "This is the new kitchen - maid, mother. [/]
[Narrator] ""Ah, is it indeed?"said Mrs. Latch looking up from the tray of tartlets which she had taken from the oven and was filling with jam. [/]
[Narrator] Esther noticed the likeness that Mrs. Latch bore to her son. [/]
[Narrator] The hair was iron grey, and, as in William's face, the nose was the most prominent feature. [/]
[Narrator] "I suppose you'll tell me the train was late?""Yes, mother, the train was a quarter of an hour late,"William chimed in. [/]
[William] "I did n't ask you, you idle, lazy, good - for - nothing vagabond. [/]
[William] I suppose it was you who kept the girl all this time. [/]
[William] Six people coming to dinner, and I've been the whole day without a kitchen - maid. [/]
[William] If Margaret Gale had n't come down to help me, I do n't know where we should be; as it is, the dinner will be late. [/]
[William] "The two housemaids, both in print dresses, stood listening. [/]
[Narrator] Esther's face clouded, and when Mrs. Latch told her to take her things off and set to and prepare the vegetables, so that she might see what she was made of, Esther did not answer at once. [/]
[Esther] She turned away, saying under her breath,"I must change my dress, and my box has not come up from the station yet.""You can tuck your dress up, and Margaret Gale will lend you her apron. [/]
[Esther] "Esther hesitated. [/]
[Esther] "What you've got on do n't look as if it could come to much damage. [/]
[Esther] Come, now, set to. [/]
[Esther] "The housemaids burst into loud laughter, and then a sullen look of dogged obstinacy passed over and settled on Esther's face, even to the point of visibly darkening the white and rose complexion. [/]
[Narrator] II A sloping roof formed one end of the room, and through a broad, single pane the early sunlight fell across a wall papered with blue and white flowers. [/]
[Narrator] Print dresses hung over the door. [/]
[Narrator] On the wall were two pictures -- a girl with a basket of flowers, the coloured supplement of an illustrated newspaper, and an old and dilapidated last century print. [/]
[Narrator] On the chimney - piece there were photographs of the Gale family in Sunday clothes, and the green vases that Sarah had given Margaret on her birthday. [/]
[Narrator] And in a low, narrow iron bed, pushed close against the wall in the full glare of the sunlight, Esther lay staring half - awake, her eyes open but still dim with dreams. [/]
[Narrator] She looked at the clock. [/]
[Narrator] It was not yet time to get up, and she raised her arms as if to cross them behind her head, but a sudden remembrance of yesterday arrested her movement, and a sudden shadow settled on her face. [/]
[Narrator] She had refused to prepare the vegetables. [/]
[Narrator] She had n't answered, and the cook had turned her out of the kitchen. [/]
[Narrator] She had rushed from the house under the momentary sway of hope that she might succeed in walking back to London; but William had overtaken her in the avenue, he had expostulated with her, he had refused to allow her to pass. [/]
[Narrator] She had striven to tear herself from him, and, failing, had burst into tears. [/]
[Narrator] However, he had been kind, and at last she had allowed him to lead her back, and all the time he had filled her ears with assurances that he would make it all right with his mother. [/]
[Narrator] But Mrs. Latch had closed her kitchen against her, and she had had to go to her room. [/]
[Narrator] Even if they paid her fare back to London, how was she to face her mother? [/]
[Narrator] What would father say? [/]
[Narrator] He would drive her from the house. [/]
[Narrator] But she had done nothing wrong. [/]
[Narrator] Why did cook insult her? [/]
[Narrator] As she pulled on her stockings she stopped and wondered if she should awake Margaret Gale. [/]
[Narrator] Margaret's bed stood in the shadow of the obliquely falling wall; and she lay heavily, one arm thrown forward, her short, square face raised to the light. [/]
[Narrator] She slept so deeply that for a moment Esther felt afraid. [/]
[Narrator] Suddenly the eyes opened, and Margaret looked at her vaguely, as if out of eternity. [/]
[Margaret] Raising her hands to her eyes she said--"What time is it?""It has just gone six." [/]
[Margaret] "Then there's plenty of time; we need n't be down before seven. [/]
[Margaret] You get on with your dressing; there's no use in my getting up till you are done -- we'd be tumbling over each other. [/]
[Margaret] This is no room to put two girls to sleep in -- one glass not much bigger than your hand. [/]
[Margaret] You'll have to get your box under your bed. .. . [/]
[Margaret] In my last place I had a beautiful room with a Brussels carpet, and a marble washstand. [/]
[Margaret] I would n't stay here three days if it weren't----"The girl laughed and turned lazily over. [/]
[Narrator] Esther did not answer. [/]
[Margaret] "Now, is n't it a grubby little room to put two girls to sleep in? [/]
[Margaret] What was your last place like?"Esther answered that she had hardly been in service before. [/]
[Narrator] Margaret was too much engrossed in her own thoughts to notice the curtness of the answer. [/]
[Margaret] "There's only one thing to be said for Woodview, and that is the eating; we have anything we want, and we'd have more than we want if it were n't for the old cook: she must have her little bit out of everything and she cuts us short in our bacon in the morning. [/]
[Margaret] But that reminds me! [/]
[Margaret] You have set the cook against you; you'll have to bring her over to your side if you want to remain here. [/]
[Margaret] ""Why should I be asked to wash up the moment I came in the house, before even I had time to change my dress. [/]
[Margaret] ""It was hard on you. [/]
[Margaret] She always gets as much as she can out of her kitchen - maid. [/]
[Margaret] But last night she was pressed, there was company to dinner. [/]
[Margaret] I'd have lent you an apron, and the dress you had on was n't of much account." [/]
[Margaret] "It is n't because a girl is poor----""Oh, I did n't mean that; I know well enough what it is to be hard up. [/]
[Margaret] "Margaret clasped her stays across her plump figure and walked to the door for her dress. [/]
[Narrator] She was a pretty girl, with a snub nose and large, clear eyes. [/]
[Narrator] Her hair was lighter in tone than Esther's, and she had brushed it from her forehead so as to obviate the defect of her face, which was too short. [/]
[Narrator] Esther was on her knees saying her prayers when Margaret turned to the light to button her boots. [/]
[Margaret] "Well, I never!" [/]
[Narrator] she exclaimed. [/]
[Margaret] "Do you think prayers any good?"Esther looked up angrily. [/]
[Esther] "I do n't want to say anything against saying prayers, but I would n't before the others if I was you -- they'll chaff dreadful, and call you Creeping Jesus. [/]
[Narrator] ""Oh, Margaret, I hope they wo n't do anything so wicked. [/]
[I] But I am afraid I sha n't be long here, so it does n't matter what they think of _ me _. [/]
[I] "When they got downstairs they opened the windows and doors, and Margaret took Esther round, showing her where the things were kept, and telling her for how many she must lay the table. [/]
[Narrator] At that moment a number of boys and men came clattering up the passage. [/]
[Narrator] They cried to Esther to hurry up, declaring that they were late. [/]
[Narrator] Esther did not know who they were, but she served them as best she might. [/]
[Narrator] They breakfasted hastily and rushed away to the stables; and they had not been long gone when the squire and his son Arthur appeared in the yard. [/]
[Narrator] The Gaffer, as he was called, was a man of about medium height. [/]
[Narrator] He wore breeches and gaiters, and in them his legs seemed grotesquely thick. [/]
[Narrator] His son was a narrow - chested, undersized young man, absurdly thin and hatchet - faced. [/]
[Narrator] He was also in breeches and gaiters, and to his boots were attached long - necked spurs. [/]
[Narrator] His pale yellow hair gave him a somewhat ludicrous appearance, as he stood talking to his father, but the moment he prepared to get into the saddle he seemed quite different. [/]
[Narrator] He rode a beautiful chestnut horse, a little too thin, Esther thought, and the ugly little boys were mounted on horses equally thin. [/]
[Narrator] The squire rode a stout grey cob, and he watched the chestnut, and was also interested in the brown horse that walked with its head in the air, pulling at the smallest of all the boys, a little freckled, red - headed fellow. [/]
[ALittleFreckledRedHeadedFellow] "That's Silver Braid, the brown horse, the one that the Demon is riding; the chestnut is Bayleaf, Ginger is riding him: he won the City and Suburban. [/]
[ALittleFreckledRedHeadedFellow] Oh, we did have a fine time then, for we all had a bit on. [/]
[ALittleFreckledRedHeadedFellow] The betting was twenty to one, and I won twelve and six pence. [/]
[ALittleFreckledRedHeadedFellow] Grover won thirty shillings. [/]
[ALittleFreckledRedHeadedFellow] They say that John -- that's the butler -- won a little fortune; but he is so close no one knows what he has on. [/]
[ALittleFreckledRedHeadedFellow] Cook would n't have anything on; she says that betting is the curse of servants -- you know what is said, that it was through betting that Mrs. Latch's husband got into trouble. [/]
[ALittleFreckledRedHeadedFellow] He was steward here, you know, in the late squire's time. [/]
[ALittleFreckledRedHeadedFellow] "Then Margaret told all she had heard on the subject. [/]
[Narrator] The late Mr. Latch had been a confidential steward, and large sums of money were constantly passing through his hands for which he was never asked for any exact account. [/]
[Narrator] Contrary to all expectation, Marksman was beaten for the Chester Cup, and the squire's property was placed under the charge of a receiver. [/]
[Narrator] Under the new management things were gone into more closely, and it was then discovered that Mr. Latch's accounts were incapable of satisfactory explanation. [/]
[Narrator] The defeat of Marksman had hit Mr. Latch as hard as it had hit the squire, and to pay his debts of honour he had to take from the money placed in his charge, confidently hoping to return it in a few months. [/]
[Narrator] The squire's misfortunes anticipated the realization of his intentions; proceedings were threatened, but were withdrawn when Mrs. Latch came forward with all her savings and volunteered to forego her wages for a term of years. [/]
[Narrator] Old Latch died soon after, some lucky bets set the squire on his legs again, the matter was half forgotten, and in the next generation it became the legend of the Latch family. [/]
[Narrator] But to Mrs. Latch it was an incurable grief, and to remove her son from influences which, in her opinion, had caused his father's death, Mrs. Latch had always refused Mr. Barfield's offers to do something for William. [/]
[Narrator] It was against her will that he had been taught to ride; but to her great joy he soon grew out of all possibility of becoming a jockey. [/]
[Narrator] She had then placed him in an office in Brighton; but the young man's height and shape marked him out for livery, and Mrs. Latch was pained when Mr. Barfield proposed it. [/]
[Margaret] "Why can not they leave me my son?" [/]
[Narrator] she cried; for it seemed to her that in that hateful cloth, buttons and cockade, he would be no more her son, and she could not forget what the Latches had been long ago. [/]
[Narrator] "I believe there's going to be a trial this morning,"said Margaret;"Silver Braid was stripped -- you noticed that -- and Ginger always rides in the trials.""I do n't know what a trial is,"said Esther. [/]
[Esther] "They are not carriage - horses, are they? [/]
[Esther] They look too slight. [/]

[Narrator] Esther Waters came from Barnstaple. [/]
[Narrator] She had been brought up in the strictness of the Plymouth Brethren, and her earliest memories were of prayers, of narrow, peaceful family life. [/]
[Narrator] This early life had lasted till she was ten years old. [/]
[Narrator] Then her father died. [/]
[Narrator] He had been a house - painter, but in early youth he had been led into intemperance by some wild companions. [/]
[Narrator] He was often not in a fit state to go to work, and one day the fumes of the beer he had drunk overpowered him as he sat in the strong sunlight on his scaffolding. [/]
[TheBrethren] In the hospital he called upon God to relieve him of his suffering; then the Brethren said,"You never thought of God before. [/]
[TheBrethren] Be patient, your health is coming back; it is a present from God; you would like to know Him and thank Him from the bottom of your heart? [/]
[TheBrethren] "John Waters'heart was touched. [/]
[Narrator] He became one of the Brethren, renouncing those companions who refused to follow into the glory of God. [/]
[Narrator] His conversion and subsequent grace won for him the sympathies of Mary Thornby. [/]
[Narrator] But Mary's father would not consent to the marriage unless John abandoned his dangerous trade of house - painter. [/]
[Narrator] John Waters consented to do this, and old James Thornby, who had made a competence in the curiosity line, offered to make over his shop to the young couple on certain conditions; these conditions were accepted, and under his father - in - law's direction John drove a successful trade in old glass, old jewellery, and old furniture. [/]
[TheBrethren] The Brethren liked not this trade, and they often came to John to speak with him on the subject, and their words were----"Of course this is between you and the Lord, but these things"(pointing to the old glass and jewellery)"often are but snares for the feet, and lead weaker brethren into temptation. [/]
[TheBrethren] Of course, it is between you and the Lord. [/]
[TheBrethren] "So John Waters was tormented with scruples concerning the righteousness of his trade, but his wife's gentle voice and eyes, and the limitations that his accident, from which he had never wholly recovered, had set upon his life, overruled his scruples, and he remained until he died a dealer in artistic ware, eliminating, however, from his dealings those things to which the Brethren most strongly objected. [/]
[Narrator] When he died his widow strove to carry on the business, but her father, who was now a confirmed invalid, could not help her. [/]
[Narrator] In the following year she lost both her parents. [/]
[Narrator] Many changes were taking place in Barnstaple, new houses were being built, a much larger and finer shop had been opened in the more prosperous end of the town, and Mrs. Waters found herself obliged to sell her business for almost nothing, and marry again. [/]
[Narrator] Children were born of this second marriage in rapid succession, the cradle was never empty, and Esther was spoken of as the little nurse. [/]
[Narrator] Her great solicitude was for her poor mother, who had lost her health, whose blood was impoverished by constant child - bearing. [/]
[Narrator] Mother and daughter were seen in the evenings, one with a baby at her breast, the other with an eighteen months old child in her arms. [/]
[Narrator] Esther did not dare leave her mother, and to protect her she gave up school, and this was why she had never learnt how to read. [/]
[Narrator] One of the many causes of quarrel between Mrs. Saunders and her husband was her attendance at prayer - meetings when he said she should be at home minding her children. [/]
[HerHusband] He used to accuse her of carrying on with the Scripture - readers, and to punish her he would say,"This week I'll spend five bob more in the public -- that'll teach you, if beating wo n't, that I do n't want none of your hypocritical folk hanging round my place." [/]
[Narrator] So it befell the Saunders family to have little to eat; and Esther often wondered how she should get a bit of dinner for her sick mother and her hungry little brothers and sisters. [/]
[Narrator] Once they passed nearly thirty hours without food. [/]
[Narrator] She called them round her, and knelt down amid them: they prayed that God might help them; and their prayers were answered, for at half - past twelve a Scripture lady came in with flowers in her hands. [/]
[Narrator] She asked Mrs. Saunders how her appetite was. [/]
[Narrator] Mrs. Saunders answered that it was more than she could afford, for there was nothing to eat in the house. [/]
[Narrator] Then the Scripture lady gave them eighteen pence, and they all knelt down and thanked God together. [/]
[Narrator] But although Saunders spent a great deal of his money in the public - house, he rarely got drunk and always kept his employment. [/]
[Narrator] He was a painter of engines, a first - rate hand, earning good money, from twenty - five to thirty shillings a week. [/]
[Narrator] He was a proud man, but so avaricious that he stopped at nothing to get money. [/]

[Narrator] She was n't one of his children, he had quite enough of his own. [/]

[Narrator] A dangerous point is this in a young girl's life. [/]

[/]

[Narrator] The avenue was full of wind and rain; the branches creaked dolefully overhead; the lane was drenched, and the bare fields were fringed with white mist, and the houses seemed very desolate by the bleak sea; and the girl's soul was desolate as the landscape. [/]
[Narrator] She had come to Woodview to escape the suffering of a home which had become unendurable, and she was going back in circumstances a hundred times worse than those in which she had left it, and she was going back with the memory of the happiness she had lost. [/]

[Narrator] It was now just after two o'clock. [/]
[Narrator] The baby was fast asleep, and would want nothing for three or four hours. [/]
[Narrator] It would be well for Esther to put on her hat and jacket and go off at once. [/]
[Narrator] Mrs. Jones gave her the address of a respectable woman who used to take charge of children. [/]
[Narrator] But this woman was nursing twins, and could not possibly undertake the charge of another baby. [/]
[Narrator] And Esther visited many streets, always failing for one reason or another. [/]
[Narrator] At last she found herself in Wandsworth, in a battered tumble - down little street, no thoroughfare, only four houses and a coal - shed. [/]
[Narrator] Broken wooden palings stood in front of the small area into which descent was made by means of a few wooden steps. [/]
[Narrator] The wall opposite seemed to be the back of some stables, and in the area of No. 3 three little mites were playing. [/]
[Narrator] The baby was tied in a chair, and a short fat woman came out of the kitchen at Esther's call, her dirty apron sloping over her high stomach, and her pale brown hair twisted into a knot at the top of her head. [/]

[Esther] It ca n't be done!" [/]

[Narrator] It took her many omnibuses to get home, and it was quite dark when she pushed the door to. [/]

[Narrator] The thought of murder went with the night, and she enjoyed the journey to Wandsworth. [/]
[Narrator] Her baby laughed and cooed, and was much admired in the omnibus, and the little street where Mrs. Spires lived seemed different. [/]
[Narrator] A cart of hay was being unloaded, and this gave the place a pleasant rural air. [/]
[Narrator] Mrs. Spires, too, was cleaner, tidier; Esther no longer disliked her; she had a nice little cot ready for the baby, and he seemed so comfortable in it that Esther did not feel the pangs at parting which she had expected to feel. [/]
[Narrator] She would see him in a few weeks, and in those weeks she would be richer. [/]
[Narrator] It seemed quite wonderful to earn so much money in so short a time. [/]
[Narrator] She had had a great deal of bad luck, but her luck seemed to have turned at last. [/]
[Narrator] So engrossed was she in the consideration of her good fortune that she nearly forgot to get out of her'bus at Charing Cross, and had it not been for the attention of the conductor might have gone on, she did not know where -- perhaps to Clerkenwell, or may be to Islington. [/]
[Narrator] When the second'bus turned into Oxford Street she got out, not wishing to spend more money than was necessary. [/]

[Narrator] And later in the evening Mrs. Rivers came down from her drawing - room to see that Esther's supper was all right, and not satisfied with the handsome fare that had been laid before her child's nurse, she went into the kitchen and gave strict orders that the meat for the future was not to be quite so much cooked. [/]
[Narrator] Henceforth it seemed to Esther that she was eating all day. [/]
[Narrator] The food was doubtless necessary after the great trial of the flesh she had been through, likewise pleasant after her long abstinences. [/]
[Narrator] She grew happy in the tide of new blood flowing in her veins, and might easily have abandoned herself in the seduction of these carnal influences. [/]
[Narrator] But her moral nature was of tough fibre, and made mute revolt. [/]
[Narrator] Such constant mealing did not seem natural, and the obtuse brain of this lowly servant - girl was perplexed. [/]
[Narrator] Her self - respect was wounded; she hated her position in this house, and sought consolation in the thought that she was earning good money for her baby. [/]
[Narrator] She noticed, too, that she never was allowed out alone, and that her walks were limited to just sufficient exercise to keep her in health. [/]

[MrsRivers] "XIX The moment Esther got out of the house in Curzon Street she felt in her pocket for her money. [/]
[Narrator] She had only a few pence; enough for her'bus fare, however, and her thoughts did not go further. [/]
[Narrator] She was absorbed by one desire, how to save her child -- how to save him from Mrs. Spires, whom she vaguely suspected; from the world, which called him a bastard and denied to him the right to live. [/]

[Narrator] And for seventeen long hours every day, for two hundred and thirty hours every fortnight, she washed, she scrubbed, she cooked, she ran errands, with never a moment that she might call her own. [/]

[Narrator] He had complained that morning that she did not keep his room sufficiently clean! [/]
[Narrator] It was a carefully - laid plan, he was watching her all the while, and no doubt thought that it was his own indiscretion that had prevented her from falling into the snare. [/]
[Narrator] Without a word Esther dropped the half - crown at his feet and returned to her work; and all the time she remained in her present situation she persistently refused to speak to him; she brought him what he asked for, but never answered him, even with a Yes or No. It was during the few minutes'rest after dinner that the burden of the day pressed heaviest upon her; then a painful weariness grew into her limbs, and it seemed impossible to summon strength and will to beat carpets or sweep down the stairs. [/]

[Narrator] She was an unfortunate girl, and had missed all her chances. [/]

[Narrator] The fight between circumstances and character had gone till now in favour of character, but circumstances must call up no further forces against character. [/]
[Narrator] A hair would turn the scale either way. [/]

[Narrator] She had determined on her plans some three weeks before her month's warning would expire, and henceforth the mountainous days of her servitude drew out interminably, seeming more than ever exhausting, and the longing in her heart to be free at times rose to her head, and her brain turned as if in delirium. [/]
[Narrator] Every time she sat down to a meal she remembered she was so many hours nearer to rest -- a fortnight's rest -- she could not afford more; but in her present slavery that fortnight seemed at once as a paradise and an eternity. [/]
[Narrator] Her only fear was that her health might give way, and that she would be laid up during the time she intended for rest -- personal rest. [/]
[Narrator] Her baby was lost sight of. [/]
[Narrator] Even a mother demands something in return for her love, and in the last year Jackie had taken much and given nothing. [/]
[Narrator] But when she opened Mrs. Lewis's door he came running to her, calling her Mummie; and the immediate preference he showed for her, climbing on her knees instead of on Mrs. Lewis's, was a fresh sowing of love in the mother's heart. [/]
[Narrator] They were in the midst of those few days of sunny weather which come in January, deluding us so with their brightness and warmth that we look round for roses and are astonished to see the earth bare of flowers. [/]
[Narrator] And these bright afternoons Esther spent entirely with Jackie. [/]
[Narrator] At the top of the hill their way led through a narrow passage between a brick wall and a high paling. [/]
[Narrator] She had always to carry him through this passage, for the ground there was sloppy and dirty, and the child wanted to stop to watch the pigs through the chinks in the boards. [/]

[Narrator] Behind the street were low - lying, shiftless fields, intersected with broken hedges. [/]

[Narrator] The evenings, too, were charming. [/]
[Narrator] When the candles were lighted, and tea was on the table, Esther sat with the dozing child on her knee, looking into the flickering fire, her mind a reverie, occasionally broken by the homely talk of her companion; and when the baby was laid in his cot she took up her sewing -- she was making herself a new dress; or else the great kettle was steaming on the hob, and the women stood over the washing - tubs. [/]
[Narrator] On the following evening they worked on either side of the ironing - table, the candle burning brightly and their vague woman's chatter sounding pleasant in the hush of the little cottage. [/]
[Narrator] A little after nine they were in bed, and so the days went softly, like happy, trivial dreams. [/]
[Narrator] It was not till the end of the third week that Mrs. Lewis would hear of Esther looking out for another place. [/]

[Narrator] Hers is an heroic adventure if one considers it -- a mother's fight for the life of her child against all the forces that civilisation arrays against the lowly and the illegitimate. [/]
[Narrator] She is in a situation to - day, but on what security does she hold it? [/]
[Narrator] She is strangely dependent on her own health, and still more upon the fortunes and the personal caprice of her employers; and she realised the perils of her life when an outcast mother at the corner of the street, stretching out of her rags a brown hand and arm, asked alms for the sake of the little children. [/]

[Narrator] XXI [/]
[Narrator] It was the beginning of August, and London yawned in every street; the dust blew unslaked, and a little cloud curled and disappeared over the crest of the hill at Hyde Park Corner; the streets and St. George's Place looked out with blind, white eyes; and in the deserted Park the trees tossed their foliage restlessly, as if they wearied and missed the fashion of their season. [/]
[Narrator] And all through Park Lane and Mayfair, caretakers and gaunt cats were the traces that the caste on which Esther depended had left of its departed presence. [/]
[Narrator] She was coming from the Alexandra Hotel, where she had heard a kitchen - maid was wanted. [/]
[Narrator] Mrs. Lewis had urged her to wait until people began to come back to town. [/]
[Narrator] Good situations were rarely obtainable in the summer months; it would be bad policy to take a bad one, even if it were only for a while. [/]
[Narrator] Besides, she had saved a little money, and, feeling that she required a rest, had determined to take this advice. [/]
[Narrator] But as luck would have it Jackie fell ill before she had been at Dulwich a week. [/]

[/]

[Narrator] They came up the long road from Fulham, with white dresses floating from their hips, and feather boas waving a few inches from the pavement. [/]
[Narrator] But through this elegant disguise Esther could pick out the servant - girls. [/]
[Narrator] Their stories were her story. [/]
[Narrator] They had been deserted, as she had been; and perhaps each had a child to support, only they had not been so lucky as she had been in finding situations. [/]
[Narrator] But now luck seemed to have deserted her. [/]
[Narrator] It was the middle of September and she had not yet been able to find the situation she wanted; and it had become more and more distressing to her to refuse sixteen pound a year. [/]
[Narrator] She had calculated it all out, and nothing less than eighteen pound was of any use to her. [/]
[Narrator] With eighteen pound and a kind mistress who would give her an old dress occasionally she could do very well. [/]
[Narrator] But if she did n't find these two pounds she did not know what she should do. [/]
[Narrator] She might drag on for a time on sixteen pound, but such wages would drive her in the end into the workhouse. [/]
[Narrator] If it were not for the child! [/]
[Narrator] But she would never desert her darling boy, who loved her so dearly, come what might. [/]
[Narrator] A sudden imagination let her see him playing in the little street, waiting for her to come home, and her love for him went to her head like madness. [/]
[Narrator] She wondered at herself; it seemed almost unnatural to love anything as she did this child. [/]
[Narrator] Then, in a shiver of fear, determined to save her'bus fare, she made her way through Leicester Square. [/]
[Narrator] She was a good - looking girl, who hastened her steps when addressed by a passer - by or crossed the roadway in sullen indignation, and who looked in contempt on the silks and satins which turned into the Empire, and she seemed to lose heart utterly. [/]
[Narrator] She had been walking all day and had not tasted food since the morning, and the weakness of the flesh brought a sudden weakness of the spirit. [/]
[Narrator] She felt that she could struggle no more, that the whole world was against her -- she felt that she must have food and drink and rest. [/]
[Narrator] All this London tempted her, and the cup was at her lips. [/]
[Narrator] A young man in evening clothes had spoken to her. [/]
[Narrator] His voice was soft, the look in his eyes seemed kindly. [/]
[Narrator] Thinking of the circumstances ten minutes later it seemed to her that she had intended to answer him. [/]
[Narrator] But she was now at Charing Cross. [/]
[Narrator] There was a lightness, an emptiness in her head which she could not overcome, and the crowd appeared to her like a blurred, noisy dream. [/]
[Narrator] And then the dizziness left her, and she realised the temptation she had escaped. [/]
[Narrator] Here, as in Piccadilly, she could pick out the servant girls; but here their service was yesterday's lodging - house -- poor and dissipated girls, dressed in vague clothes fixed with hazardous pins. [/]
[Narrator] Two young women strolled in front of her. [/]
[Narrator] They hung on each other's arms, talking lazily. [/]
[Narrator] They had just come out of an eating - house, and a happy digestion was in their eyes. [/]
[Narrator] The skirt on the outside was a soiled mauve, and the bodice that went with it was a soiled chocolate. [/]
[Narrator] A broken yellow plume hung out of a battered hat. [/]
[Narrator] The skirt on the inside was a dim green, and little was left of the cotton velvet jacket but the cotton. [/]
[Narrator] A girl of sixteen walking sturdily, like a little man, crossed the road, her left hand thrust deep into the pocket of her red cashmere dress. [/]
[Narrator] She wore on her shoulders a strip of beaded mantle; her hair was plaited and tied with a red ribbon. [/]
[Narrator] Corpulent women passed, their eyes liquid with invitation; and the huge bar - loafer, the man of fifty, the hooked nose and the waxed moustache, stood at the door of a restaurant, passing the women in review. [/]
[Narrator] A true London of the water's edge -- a London of theatres, music - halls, wine - shops, public - houses -- the walls painted various colours, nailed over with huge gold lettering; the pale air woven with delicate wire, a gossamer web underneath which the crowd moved like lazy flies, one half watching the perforated spire of St. Mary's, and all the City spires behind it now growing cold in the east, the other half seeing the spire of St. Martin's above the chimney - pots aloft in a sky of cream pink. [/]
[Narrator] Stalwart policemen urged along groups of slattern boys and girls; and after vulgar remonstrance these took the hint and disappeared down strange passages. [/]
[Narrator] Suddenly Esther came face to face with a woman whom she recognised as Margaret Gale. [/]
[Narrator] "What, is it you, Margaret?""Yes, it is me all right. [/]

[Narrator] Margaret told Esther the story of her misfortune. [/]
[Narrator] The Barfields were all broken up. [/]
[Narrator] They had been very unlucky racing, and when the servants got the sack Margaret had come up to London. [/]
[Narrator] She had been in several situations. [/]
[Narrator] Eventually, one of her masters had got her into trouble, his wife had turned her out neck and crop, and what was she to do? [/]

[MasterHarry] "And you left like that? [/]
[MasterHarry] Well I never! [/]
[MasterHarry] The better one behaves the worse one gets treated, and them that goes on with service find themselves in the end without as much as will buy them a Sunday dinner. [/]

[Margaret] "I ca n't go any further,"and pointing to where London seemed to end in a piece of desolate sky, she said,"I live on the other side, in Stamford Street. [/]
[Margaret] You might come and see me. [/]
[Margaret] If you ever get tired of service you'll get decent rooms there. [/]
[Margaret] "Bad weather followed fine, and under a streaming umbrella Esther went from one address to another, her damp skirts clinging about her and her boots clogged with mud. [/]
[Narrator] She looked upon the change in the weather as unfortunate, for in getting a situation so much depended on personal appearance and cheerfulness of manner; and it is difficult to seem a right and tidy girl after two miles'walk through the rain. [/]
[Narrator] One lady told Esther that she liked tall servants, another said she never engaged good - looking girls, and another place that would have suited her was lost through unconsciously answering that she was chapel. [/]
[Narrator] The lady would have nothing in her house but church. [/]

[Narrator] Ketley and Journeyman held widely different views regarding the best means of backing horses. [/]
[Narrator] Ketley was preoccupied with dreams and omens; Journeyman, a clerk in the parish registry office, studied public form; he was guided by it in all his speculations, and paid little heed to the various rumours always afloat regarding private trials. [/]
[Narrator] Public form he admitted did not always come out right, but if a man had a headpiece and could remember all the running, public form was good enough to follow. [/]

[Narrator] XXXV Lacking a parlour on the ground floor for the use of special customers, William had arranged a room upstairs where they could smoke and drink. [/]

[Narrator] He thought that it would be safer. [/]

[Narrator] Nevertheless the room upstairs had proved a success. [/]
[Narrator] A man spent more money if he had a room where he could sit quietly among his friends than he would seated on a high stool in a public bar, jostled and pushed about; so it had come to be considered a sort of club room; and a large part of the neighbourhood came there to read the papers, to hear and discuss the news. [/]
[Narrator] And specially useful it had proved to Journeyman and Stack. [/]
[Narrator] Neither was now in employment; they were now professional backers; and from daylight to dark they wandered from public - house to public - house, from tobacconist to barber's shop, in the search of tips, on the quest of stable information regarding the health of the horses and their trials. [/]
[TheIndefatigableTipster] But the room upstairs at the"King's Head"was the centre of their operations. [/]
[Narrator] Stack was the indefatigable tipster, Journeyman was the scientific student of public form. [/]
[Narrator] His memory was prodigious, and it enabled him to note an advantage in the weights which would escape an ordinary observer. [/]
[Narrator] He often picked out horses which, if they did not actually win, nearly always stood at a short price in the betting before the race. [/]

[Narrator] Barbers and their assistants, cabmen, scene - shifters, if there was an afternoon performance at the theatre, servants out of situation and servants escaped from their service for an hour, petty shopkeepers, the many who grow weary of the scant livelihood that work brings them, came there. [/]
[Narrator] Eleven o'clock! [/]
[Narrator] In another hour the bar and the room upstairs would be crowded. [/]

[Narrator] Wanderer had beaten Brick at ten pounds. [/]

[Narrator] "XXXVI One afternoon Fred Parsons came into the bar of the"King's Head." [/]

[Narrator] I confess that I do not see why we should condone the prisoner's offence because it was committed for the sake of obtaining money for gambling purposes. [/]
[Narrator] Indeed, it seems to me a reason for dealing heavily with the offence. [/]
[Narrator] The vice among the poorer classes is largely on the increase, and it seems to me that it is the duty of all in authority to condemn rather than to condone the evil, and to use every effort to stamp it out. [/]
[Narrator] For my part I fail to perceive any romantic element in the vice of gambling. [/]
[Narrator] It springs from the desire to obtain wealth without work, in other words, without payment; work, whether in the past or the present, is the natural payment for wealth, and any wealth that is obtained without work is in a measure a fraud committed upon the community. [/]
[Narrator] Poverty, despair, idleness, and every other vice spring from gambling as naturally, and in the same profusion, as weeds from barren land. [/]
[Narrator] Drink, too, is gambling's firmest ally. [/]
[GamblingSFirmestAlly] "At this moment a certain dryness in his Lordship's throat reminded him of the pint of excellent claret that lordship always drank with his lunch, and the thought enabled lordship to roll out some excellent invective against the evils of beer and spirits. [/]
[Narrator] And lordship's losses on the horse whose name he could hardly recall helped to a forcible illustration of the theory that drink and gambling mutually uphold and enforce each other. [/]

[Narrator] The porter told her that he would try to send her box up to Woodview to - morrow. .. . [/]
[Narrator] That was the way to Woodview, right up the lane. [/]
[Narrator] She could not miss it. [/]
[Narrator] She would find the lodge gate behind that clump of trees. [/]
[Narrator] And thinking how she could get her box to Woodview that evening, she looked at the barren strip of country lying between the downs and the shingle beach. [/]
[Narrator] The little town clamped about its deserted harbour seemed more than ever like falling to pieces like a derelict vessel, and when Esther passed over the level crossing she noticed that the line of little villas had not increased; they were as she had left them eighteen years ago, laurels, iron railing, antimacassars. [/]
[Narrator] It was about eighteen years ago, on a beautiful June day, that she had passed up this lane for the first time. [/]
[Narrator] At the very spot she was now passing she had stopped to wonder if she would be able to keep the place of kitchen - maid. [/]
[Narrator] She remembered regretting that she had not a new dress; she had hoped to be able to brighten up the best of her cotton prints with a bit of red ribbon. [/]
[Narrator] The sun was shining, and she had met William leaning over the paling in the avenue smoking his pipe. [/]
[Narrator] Eighteen years had gone by, eighteen years of labour, suffering, disappointment. [/]
[Narrator] A great deal had happened, so much that she could not remember it all. [/]
[Narrator] The situations she had been in; her life with that dear good soul, Miss Rice, then Fred Parsons, then William again; her marriage, the life in the public - house, money lost and money won, heart - breakings, death, everything that could happen had happened to her. [/]
[Narrator] Now it all seemed like a dream. [/]
[Narrator] But her boy remained to her. [/]
[Narrator] She had brought up her boy, thank God, she had been able to do that. [/]
[Narrator] But how had she done it? [/]
[Narrator] How often had she found herself within sight of the workhouse? [/]
[Narrator] The last time was no later than last week. [/]
[Narrator] Last week it had seemed to her that she would have to accept the workhouse. [/]
[Narrator] But she had escaped, and now here she was back at the very point from which she started, going back to Woodview, going back to Mrs. Barfield's service. [/]
[Narrator] William's illness and his funeral had taken Esther's last few pounds away from her, and when she and Jack came back from the cemetery she found that she had broken into her last sovereign. [/]
[Narrator] She clasped him to her bosom -- he was a tall boy of fifteen -- and burst into tears. [/]
[Narrator] But she did not tell him what she was crying for. [/]
[Esther] She did not say,"God only knows how we shall find bread to eat next week;"she merely said, wiping away her tears,"We ca n't afford to live here any longer. [/]
[Esther] It's too expensive for us now that father's gone." [/]
[Narrator] And they went to live in a slum for three - and - sixpence a week. [/]
[Narrator] If she had been alone in the world she would have gone into a situation, but she could not leave the boy, and so she had to look out for charing. [/]
[Narrator] It was hard to have to come down to this, particularly when she remembered that she had had a house and a servant of her own; but there was nothing for it but to look out for some charing, and get along as best she could until Jack was able to look after himself. [/]
[Narrator] But the various scrubbings and general cleaning that had come her way had been so badly paid that she soon found that she could not make both ends meet. [/]
[Narrator] She would have to leave her boy and go out as a general servant. [/]
[Narrator] And as her necessities were pressing, she accepted a situation in a coffee - shop in the London Road. [/]
[Narrator] She would give all her wages to Jack, seven shillings a week, and he would have to live on that. [/]
[Narrator] So long as she had her health she did not mind. [/]
[Narrator] It was a squat brick building with four windows that looked down on the pavement with a short - sighted stare. [/]
[Esther] On each window was written in letters of white enamel,"Well - aired beds." [/]
[Narrator] A board nailed to a post by the side - door announced that tea and coffee were always ready. [/]
[Narrator] On the other side of the sign was an upholsterer's, and the vulgar brightness of the Brussels carpets seemed in keeping with the slop - like appearance of the coffeehouse. [/]
[Narrator] Sometimes a workman came in the morning; a couple more might come in about dinner - time. [/]
[Narrator] Sometimes they took rashers and bits of steak out of their pockets. [/]
[ACoupleMore] "Wo n't you cook this for me, missis?" [/]
[Narrator] But it was not until about nine in the evening that the real business of the house began, and it continued till one, when the last straggler knocked for admittance. [/]
[Narrator] The house lived on its beds. [/]
[Narrator] The best rooms were sometimes let for eight shillings a night, and there were four beds which were let at fourpence a night in the cellar under the area where Esther stood by the great copper washing sheets, blankets, and counterpanes, when she was not cleaning the rooms upstairs. [/]
[Narrator] There was a double - bedded room underneath the kitchen, and over the landings, wherever a space could be found, the landlord, who was clever at carpentering work, had fitted up some sort of closet place that could be let as a bedroom. [/]
[Narrator] The house was a honeycomb. [/]
[Narrator] The landlord slept under the roof, and a corner had been found for his housekeeper, a handsome young woman, at the end of the passage. [/]
[Narrator] Esther and the children -- the landlord was a widower -- slept in the coffee - room upon planks laid across the tops of the high backs of the benches where the customers mealed. [/]
[Narrator] Mattresses and bedding were laid on these planks and the sleepers lay, their faces hardly two feet from the ceiling. [/]
[Narrator] Esther slept with the baby, a little boy of five; the two big boys slept at the other end of the room by the front door. [/]
[Narrator] The eldest was about fifteen, but he was only half - witted; and he helped in the housework, and could turn down the beds and see quicker than any one if the occupant had stolen sheet or blanket. [/]
[Narrator] Esther always remembered how he would raise himself up in bed in the early morning, rub the glass, and light a candle so that he could be seen from below. [/]
[Narrator] He shook his head if every bed was occupied, or signed with his fingers the prices of the beds if they had any to let. [/]
[Narrator] The landlord was a tall, thin man, with long features and hair turning grey. [/]
[Narrator] He was very quiet, and Esther was surprised one night at the abruptness with which he stopped a couple who were going upstairs. [/]

[Narrator] It was very soon after that Esther had her mother's books stolen from her. [/]
[Narrator] They had not been doing much business, and she had been put to sleep in one of the bedrooms. [/]
[Narrator] The room was suddenly wanted, and she had no time to move all her things, and when she went to make up the room she found that her mother's books and a pair of jet earrings that Fred had given her had been stolen. [/]
[Narrator] She could do nothing; the couple who had occupied the room were far away by this time. [/]
[Narrator] There was no hope of ever recovering her books and earrings, and the loss of these things caused her a great deal of unhappiness. [/]
[Narrator] The only little treasure she possessed were those earrings; now they were gone, she realised how utterly alone she was in the world. [/]
[Narrator] If her health were to break down to - morrow she would have to go to the workhouse. [/]
[Narrator] What would become of her boy? [/]
[Narrator] She was afraid to think; thinking did no good. [/]
[Narrator] She must not think, but must just work on, washing the bedclothes until she could wash no longer. [/]

[Narrator] Never, not even in the house in Chelsea, had she had such hard work, and she was not as strong now as she was then. [/]
[Narrator] But her courage did not give way until one Sunday Jack came to tell her that the people who employed him had sold their business. [/]
[Narrator] Then a strange weakness came over her. [/]
[Narrator] She thought of the endless week of work that awaited her in the cellar, the great copper on the fire, the heaps of soiled linen in the corner, the steam rising from the wash - tub, and she felt she had not sufficient strength to get through another week of such work. [/]
[Narrator] She looked at her son with despair in her eyes. [/]

[Narrator] XLVI Esther seemed to have quite naturally accepted Woodview as a final stage. [/]

[Narrator] XLVII Days, weeks, months passed away, and the two women came to live more and more like friends and less like mistress and maid. [/]
[Esther] Not that Esther ever failed to use the respectful"ma'am"when she addressed her mistress, nor did they ever sit down to a meal at the same table. [/]

[Narrator] In the evening they sat in the library sewing, or Mrs. Barfield read aloud, or they talked of their sons. [/]

[Esther] One Sunday morning, after meeting, as the two women were going for their walk up the hill, Esther said--"I've a letter from my boy, ma'am. [/]
[Esther] I hope it is to tell me that he's got back to work.""I'm afraid I sha n't be able to read it, Esther. [/]
[Esther] I have n't my glasses with me.""It do n't matter, ma'am -- it'll keep.""Give it to me -- his writing is large and legible. [/]
[Esther] I think I can read it.' [/]
[Esther] My dear mother, the place I told you of in my last letter was given away, so I must go on in the toy - shop till something better turns up. [/]
[Esther] I only get six shillings a week and my tea, and ca n't quite manage on that.' [/]
[Esther] Then something -- something--'pay three and sixpence a week'--something--'bed'--something -- something.""I know, ma'am; he shares a bed with the eldest boy." [/]
[Esther] "Yes, that's it; and he wants to know if you can help him. [/]
[Esther] 'I do n't like to trouble you, mother; but it is hard for a boy to get his living in London.' [/]
[Esther] ""But I've sent him all my money. [/]
[Esther] I sha n't have any till next quarter. [/]
[Esther] ""I'll lend you some, Esther. [/]
[Esther] We ca n't leave the boy to starve. [/]
[Esther] He ca n't live on two and sixpence a week." [/]
[Esther] "You're very good, ma'am; but I do n't like to take your money. [/]
[Esther] We sha n't be able to get the garden cleared this winter. [/]
[Esther] ""We shall manage somehow, Esther. [/]
[Esther] The garden must wait. [/]
[Esther] The first thing to do is to see that your boy does n't want for food. [/]
[Esther] "The women resumed their walk up the hill. [/]
[MrsBarfield] When they reached the top Mrs. Barfield said--"I have n't heard from Mr. Arthur for months. [/]
[MrsBarfield] I envy you, Esther, those letters asking for a little money. [/]
[MrsBarfield] What's the use of money to us except to give it to our children? [/]
[MrsBarfield] Helping others, that is the only happiness. [/]
[MrsBarfield] "At the end of the coombe, under the shaws, stood the old red - tiled farmhouse in which Mrs. Barfield had been born. [/]
[Narrator] Beyond it, downlands rolled on and on, reaching half - way up the northern sky. [/]
[Narrator] Mrs. Barfield was thinking of the days when her husband used to jump off his cob and walk beside her through those gorse patches on his way to the farmhouse. [/]
[Narrator] She had come from the farmhouse beneath the shaws to go to live in an Italian house sheltered by a fringe of trees. [/]
[Narrator] That was her adventure. [/]
[Narrator] She knew it, and she turned from the view of the downs to the view of the sea. [/]
[Narrator] The plantations of Woodview touched the horizon, then the line dipped, and between the top branches of a row of elms appeared the roofs of the town. [/]
[Narrator] Over a long spider - legged bridge a train wriggled like a snake, the bleak river flowed into the harbour, and the shingle banks saved the low land from inundation. [/]
[Narrator] Then the train passed behind the square, dogmatic tower of the village church. [/]
[Narrator] Her husband lay beneath the chancel; her father, mother, all her relations, lay in the churchyard. [/]
[Narrator] She would go there in a few years. .. . [/]
[Narrator] Her daughter lay far away, far away in Egypt. [/]
[Narrator] Upon this downland all her life had been passed, all her life except the few months she had spent by her daughter's bedside in Egypt. [/]
[Narrator] She had come from that coombe, from that farmhouse beneath the shaws, and had only crossed the down. [/]
[Narrator] And this barren landscape meant as much to Esther as to her mistress. [/]
[Narrator] It was on these downs that she had walked with William. [/]
[Narrator] He had been born and bred on these downs; but he lay far away in Brompton Cemetery; it was she who had come back! [/]
[Narrator] and in her simple way she too wondered at the mystery of destiny. [/]
[Narrator] As they descended the hill Mrs. Barfield asked Esther if she ever heard of Fred Parsons. [/]
[MrsBarfield] "No, ma'am, I do n't know what's become of him. [/]
[MrsBarfield] ""And if you were to meet him again, would you care to marry him?""Marry and begin life over again! [/]
[MrsBarfield] All the worry and bother over again! [/]
[MrsBarfield] Why should I marry? --all I live for now is to see my boy settled in life. [/]
[MrsBarfield] "The women walked on in silence, passing by long ruins of stables, coach - houses, granaries, rickyards, all in ruin and decay. [/]
[Narrator] The women paused and went towards the garden; and removing some pieces of the broken gate they entered a miniature wilderness. [/]
[Narrator] The espalier apple - trees had disappeared beneath climbing weeds, and long briars had shot out from the bushes, leaving few traces of the former walks -- a damp, dismal place that the birds seemed to have abandoned. [/]
[Narrator] Of the greenhouse only some broken glass and a black broken chimney remained. [/]
[Narrator] A great elm had carried away a large portion of the southern wall, and under the dripping trees an aged peacock screamed for his lost mate. [/]
[I] "I do n't suppose that Jack will be able to find any more paying employment this winter. [/]
[I] We must send him six shillings a week; that, with what he is earning, will make twelve; he'll be able to live nicely on that. [/]
[Narrator] ""I should think he would indeed. [/]
[Narrator] But, then, what about the wages of them who was to have cleared the gardens for us?""We sha n't be able to get the whole garden cleared, but Jim will be able to get a piece ready for us to sow some spring vegetables, not a large piece, but enough for us. [/]
[I] The first thing to do will be to cut down those apple - trees. [/]
[I] I'm afraid we shall have to cut down that walnut; nothing could grow beneath it. [/]
[I] Did any one ever see such a mass of weed and briar? [/]
[I] Yet it is only about ten years since we left Woodview, and the garden was let run to waste. [/]
[I] Nature does not take long, a few years, a very few years. [/]
[I] "XLVIII All the winter the north wind roamed on the hills; many trees fell in the park, and at the end of February Woodview seemed barer and more desolate than ever; broken branches littered the roadway, and the tall trunks showed their wounds. [/]
[Narrator] The women sat over their fire in the evening listening to the blast, cogitating the work that awaited them as soon as the weather showed signs of breaking. [/]
[Narrator] Mrs. Barfield had laid by a few pounds during the winter; and the day that Jim cleared out the first piece of espalier trees she spent entirely in the garden, hardly able to take her eyes off him. [/]
[Narrator] But the pleasure of the day was in a measure spoilt for her by the knowledge that on that day her son was riding in the great steeplechase. [/]
[Narrator] She was full of fear for his safety; she did not sleep that night, and hurried down at an early hour to the garden to ask Jim for the newspaper which she had told him to bring her. [/]
[Narrator] He took some time to extract the paper from his torn pocket. [/]
[MrsBarfield] "He is n't in the first three,"said Mrs. Barfield. [/]
[MrsBarfield] "I always know that he's safe if he's in the first three. [/]
[MrsBarfield] We must turn to the account of the race to see if there were any accidents. [/]
[MrsBarfield] "She turned over the paper. [/]
[MrsBarfield] "Thank God, he's safe,"she said;"his horse ran fourth. [/]
[MrsBarfield] ""You worry yourself without cause, ma'am. [/]
[MrsBarfield] A good rider like him do n't meet with accidents. [/]
[MrsBarfield] ""The best riders are often killed, Esther. [/]
[MrsBarfield] I never have an easy moment when I hear he's going to ride in these races. [/]
[MrsBarfield] Supposing one day I were to read that he was carried back on a shutter. [/]
[MrsBarfield] ""We must n't let our thoughts run on such things, ma'am. [/]
[MrsBarfield] If a war was to break out to - morrow, what should I do? [/]
[MrsBarfield] His regiment would be ordered out. [/]
[MrsBarfield] It is sad to think that he had to enlist. [/]
[MrsBarfield] But, as he said, he could n't go on living on me any longer. [/]
[MrsBarfield] Poor boy! .. . [/]
[MrsBarfield] We must keep on working, doing the best we can for them. [/]
[MrsBarfield] There are all sorts of chances, and we can only pray that God may spare them." [/]
[MrsBarfield] "Yes, Esther, that's all we can do. [/]
[MrsBarfield] Work on, work on to the end. .. . [/]
[MrsBarfield] But your boy is coming to see you to - day." [/]
[MrsBarfield] "Yes, ma'am, he'll be here by twelve o'clock.' [/]
[MrsBarfield] ""You're luckier than I am. [/]
[MrsBarfield] I wonder if I shall ever see my boy again." [/]
[MrsBarfield] "Yes, ma'am, of course you will. [/]
[MrsBarfield] He'll come back to you right enough one of these days. [/]
[MrsBarfield] There's a good time coming; that's what I always says. .. . [/]
[MrsBarfield] And now I've got work to do in the house. [/]
[MrsBarfield] Are you going to stop here, or are you coming in with me? [/]
[MrsBarfield] It'll do you no good standing about in the wet clay. [/]
[MrsBarfield] "Mrs. Barfield smiled and nodded, and Esther paused at the broken gate to watch her mistress, who stood superintending the clearing away of ten years'growth of weeds, as much interested in the prospect of a few peas and cabbages as in former days she had been in the culture of expensive flowers. [/]
[Narrator] She stood on what remained of a gravel walk, the heavy clay clinging to her boots, watching Jim piling weeds upon his barrow. [/]
[Narrator] Would he be able to finish the plot of ground by the end of the week? [/]
[Narrator] What should they do with that great walnut - tree? [/]
[Narrator] Nothing would grow underneath it. [/]
[Narrator] Jim was afraid that he would not be able to cut it down and remove it without help. [/]
[Narrator] Mrs. Barfield suggested sawing away some of the branches, but Jim was not sure that the expedient would prove of much avail. [/]
[Narrator] In his opinion the tree took all the goodness out of the soil, and that while it stood they could not expect a very great show of vegetables. [/]
[Narrator] Mrs. Barfield asked if the sale of the tree trunk would indemnify her for the cost of cutting it down. [/]
[Narrator] Jim paused in his work, and, leaning on his spade, considered if there was any one in the town, who, for the sake of the timber, would cut the tree down and take it away for nothing. [/]
[Narrator] There ought to be some such person in town; if it came to that, Mrs. Barfield ought to receive something for the tree. [/]
[Narrator] Walnut was a valuable wood, was extensively used by cabinetmakers, and so on, until Mrs. Barfield begged him to get on with his digging. [/]
[Narrator] At twelve o'clock Esther and Mrs. Barfield walked out on the lawn. [/]
[Narrator] A loud wind came up from the sea, and it shook the evergreens as if it were angry with them. [/]
[Narrator] A rook carried a stick to the tops of the tall trees, and the women drew their cloaks about them. [/]
[Narrator] The train passed across the vista, and the women wondered how long it would take Jack to walk from the station. [/]
[Narrator] Then another rook stooped to the edge of the plantation, gathered a twig, and carried it away. [/]
[Narrator] The wind was rough; it caught the evergreens underneath and blew them out like umbrellas; the grass had not yet begun to grow, and the grey sea harmonised with the grey - green land. [/]
[Narrator] The women waited on the windy lawn, their skirts blown against their legs, keeping their hats on with difficulty. [/]
[Narrator] It was too cold for standing still. [/]
[Narrator] They turned and walked a few steps towards the house, and then looked round. [/]
[Narrator] A tall soldier came through the gate. [/]
[Narrator] He wore a long red cloak, and a small cap jauntily set on the side of his close - clipped head. [/]
[Narrator] Esther uttered a little exclamation, and ran to meet him. [/]
[Narrator] He took his mother in his arms, kissed her, and they walked towards Mrs. Barfield together. [/]
[Narrator] All was forgotten in the happiness of the moment -- the long fight for his life, and the possibility that any moment might declare him to be mere food for powder and shot. [/]
[Narrator] She was only conscious that she had accomplished her woman's work -- she had brought him up to man's estate; and that was her sufficient reward. [/]
[Narrator] What a fine fellow he was! [/]
[Narrator] She did not know he was so handsome, and blushing with pleasure and pride she glanced shyly at him out of the corners of her eyes as she introduced him to her mistress. [/]
[Esther] "This is my son, ma'am."Mrs. Barfield held out her hand to the young soldier. [/]
[MrsBarfield] "I have heard a great deal about you from your mother. [/]
[MrsBarfield] " [/]
[MrsBarfield] "And I of you, ma'am. [/]
[MrsBarfield] You've been very kind to my mother. [/]
[MrsBarfield] I do n't know how to thank you. [/]
[MrsBarfield] "And in silence they walked towards the house. [/]
